You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels, and new mechanics might be introduced.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still reliably clearing every level. Per level you are scored on action efficiency, `min(human_actions / agent_actions, 1.0)`, then squared, so once a level is solved every extra action hurts sharply.
- Use this as a test for false goals: if a value only ticks up by a fixed amount each action but never completes a level (a HUD counter, timer, or progress bar), it cannot be the objective; pursuing it means one wasted action per tick, the worst possible efficiency. The real goal is a change in the puzzle's own state that drives toward clearing the level.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is a lightweight frame view for the latest environment state.
- `current_frame` exposes only `.ascii`, `.step`, `.level`, `.shape`, and `.segmentation`.
- `current_frame.ascii` is a single newline-delimited string containing the latest board rendered with the letter-coded ARC color symbols.
- `current_frame.segmentation` parses the board into objects. It returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`.
- Each node in `segmentation['nodes']` is one 4-connected same-color object with: `id` (index, ordered top-most-left-most), `color` (ARC color character), `hash` (a signature of the object's color and shape that ignores its position -- equal hashes mean the same object regardless of where it is, so use it to track an object across frames or to spot multiple identical objects in one frame), `pixels` (cell count), `boundary` (clockwise outer-perimeter corner points as `[row, col]`), and `children` (ids of objects fully enclosed by this one).
- `segmentation['adjacency_list']` is a list of `[i, j]` node-id pairs whose objects share an edge.
- `current_frame.step` is the current environment step count.
- `current_frame.level` is the current level number.
- `current_frame.shape` is a `(rows, cols)` tuple.
- The raw numeric grid is intentionally not exposed. Use `current_frame.segmentation` as your primary view of the board -- objects, colors, shapes, containment, adjacency, and cross-frame object hashes. Use `current_frame.ascii` only to read a small, specific region; do not scan the whole board with it.
- `history` is a chronological list of action/frame snapshots.
- `history` is a Python list of objects, not a dict.
- Each history entry exposes only `.action` and `.frame`; entries are not subscriptable like `entry['action']`.
- Each `history[i].frame` is the frame after `history[i].action`; each frame exposes only `.ascii`, `.step`, `.level`, `.shape`, and `.segmentation`.
- Important history semantics: when `history` is non-empty, `history[-1].frame` is the same latest/post-action board as `current_frame`. It is not the previous board. To inspect the state before the latest action, use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` when available.
- `previous_frame` is the frame before the most recent real environment action, or `None` if no previous frame is available.
- `last_action` is the most recent real environment action name/display, or `None` before any real action.
- `last_action_frame` is the post-action frame for `last_action`; it matches `current_frame` after a real action.
- `transitions` is a chronological list of actual action transitions, excluding the initial seeded frame. Each transition exposes `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame` (alias of `.after_frame`), and `.result`.
- `last_transition` is `transitions[-1]` or `None`. Its `.result` mirrors `last_action_result`; older transitions may have an empty `.result`. For before/after diffs, compare `last_transition.before_frame` to `last_transition.after_frame`; do not compare `current_frame` to `history[-1].frame`.
- `last_action_result` is the persisted result dict from the most recent `action(...)` call. It remains available across later Python inspection calls that do not call `action(...)`, and is `{}` before any action result exists. Read transition metadata from fields/keys such as `last_action_result['board_changed']`, `last_action_result['done']`, `last_action_result['level_completed']`, `last_action_result['game_over']`, `last_action_result['reward']`, and `last_action_result['valid_actions']`.
- `valid_actions` is the current list of valid action names.
- Call `action(actions)` to execute one or more real environment actions from Python.
- Pass `action(actions)` a list like `['LEFT']` or `[{'action': 'MOUSE', 'row': 4, 'col': 7}]`.
- One action usually returns one frame, but a single action can result in a short multi-frame animation.
- After `action(actions)` returns, `current_frame`, `previous_frame`, `history`, `transitions`, `valid_actions`, and `last_action_result` are refreshed.
- Each result reports `board_changed`, plus `gameplay_changed` and `no_impact`: the harness learns the game's deterministic HUD/edge band (a moves-remaining or timer bar) online and tells you whether your action changed real gameplay (`gameplay_changed`) or only advanced that band (`no_impact` -- a wasted move, e.g. pressing into a wall). `hud_band` names the learned band when one exists (e.g. `rows 61-62`).
- `action(actions, intent=...)` defaults to `intent='explore'`: an explore action that turns out `no_impact` STOPS the rest of that batch (`stop_reason='no_impact_action'`) and returns so you re-ground, instead of wasting the queued actions on a plan the environment just disproved. Pass `intent='solve'` for a committed sequence you do not want interrupted by the no-impact check.
- The harness keeps a per-level STATE GRAPH of the canonical (HUD-excluded) boards you have visited and the actions between them. EVERY action result already gives you `state_node_id` (your node now), `graph_nodes` (count), and `untried_here` (the valid actions you have NOT yet taken from this state) -- so do NOT spend a tool-call turn re-deriving your position or local frontier; act on `untried_here` directly. The `state_graph` sandbox global is for PLANNING A ROUTE only (use it sparingly, not every turn): `.neighbors(id)`, `.tried_actions(id)`, `.distance(a,b)`, `.path(a,b)`, `.frontier()`, and `frame_from_state(id)` -> a FrameView (`.ascii`/`.segmentation`) to inspect a distant node before navigating to it.
- In `intent='explore'`, a whole action sequence that follows ONLY already-tried edges is REJECTED without executing (`stop_reason='known_action_rejected'`): it re-treads explored ground and reaches no new (state, action). You MAY route back through known / fully-explored states, but the plan must END with an untried action so it reaches new ground -- your frontier at a node is its `valid_actions` minus `state_graph.tried_actions(id)`. Use `intent='re-explore'` to deliberately traverse known states (the batch is only flagged, or hard-stopped in the strict A/B arm, if it reaches nothing new); `intent='solve'` bypasses the graph and no-impact checks entirely.
- Optional precision upgrade for that check: if you can pin down the deterministic HUD/bar exactly, write a PURE `advance_hud(frame, action) -> frame` (frame is the `.ascii` letter grid; return it with ONLY the bar advanced one step, everything else identical) and commit it in a `HUD model:` section with a fenced python code block. The harness re-verifies it against every action and, while its bar prediction holds, uses it (`no_impact_source: model`) instead of the statistical band -- you write it ONCE and it then runs free every turn with no further tokens. It only asks you to rewrite when it mispredicts (`hud_model: stale`, e.g. a refill or a new level). No HUD in this game? Don't write one -- the statistical fallback handles it (`no_impact_source: band` or `exact`).
- `ACTION7` is a valid game-specific action when it appears in `valid_actions`. Its meaning is not fixed across games; infer it from a safe probe and the returned before/after and animation metadata rather than assuming it means undo, confirm, or back.
- Every action in the result's `executed_actions` carries an `animation` record summarizing its intermediate frames (which are not visible in `current_frame`): `num_frames`, `changed`, `only_changed` (a transient flash that left the same final board), `total_cells_changed`, `cells_changed_per_frame` (per frame, where 0 is a held/dwell frame), and `objects` -- one string per connected animated region, e.g. `top-left (19,27) 4x4, 16 cells, per-frame [...]`, so two things moving at once (a box resizing while a light blinks) each keep a tight bounding box.
- Beyond the `animation` record above, two optional runtime variables expose the actual per-action frames: `last_animation` and `frame_stats`.
- `current_frame` is always the settled board after your last action -- on its own it is enough for a purely static reading, and you never have to look further.
- If you want to, `last_animation` additionally exposes every frame the engine produced for each INDIVIDUAL action of your most recent `action(...)` call: a list with one entry per action, in execution order, each with `.action` and `.frames` (intermediate animation frames plus the final frame; length 1 means that action caused no animation). After `action(['UP','UP','LEFT'])` there are three entries. All change within one entry's `.frames` is that single action animating -- read it to see per-move motion and causality that the settled frame discards.
- `frame_stats` summarizes how much THIS game has animated so far: `actions`, `animated_actions`, `mean_frames_per_action`, `max_frames`, and `recent_frame_counts` (per action, most recent last). Use it to gauge, from the game so far, whether the extra frames are likely worth reading.
- `show_animation_by_objects(action_index=0)` prints that action's animation one object at a time, each cropped to its own bounding box (labeled with the object's top-left row, col) and reduced to its distinct states -- small and legible even for a long animation. Reach for it when an action reports animated objects, especially when a small `total_cells_changed` signals a focused, meaningful animation (a box growing, a token sliding) rather than a full-board redraw.
- `show_animation_by_bbox(action_index=0, row=..., col=..., rows=..., cols=...)` prints the same animation cropped to a region YOU choose (top-left `row`,`col` plus `rows`,`cols`) -- use it to inspect a spot the object split missed. Omit `rows`/`cols` to watch the FULL animation frames.
- It is entirely your judgement whether the intermediate frames are signal or noise. Motion may be meaningful (an object sliding, a chain reaction, a knock-on effect) OR incidental (a decorative transition, a flashing timer/HUD, a cosmetic redraw). Treat multi-frame motion as a HYPOTHESIS to test against the puzzle, not a fact. Reading `last_animation` costs tokens only when you inspect it, so pull it only when you expect it to help.


Text grid context:
- This model does not receive images. Every user turn already includes the current ARC grid inline as ASCII text, using the same letter-coded color symbols as `current_frame.ascii`.
- You do not need to call the `python` tool just to read the grid for the first time each turn -- it is already in this message. Use `current_frame.segmentation` (and `.ascii` again if you need to re-read a specific region) for structured object/adjacency analysis.
- Do NOT manually count or transcribe ASCII grid characters cell-by-cell (e.g. counting columns one letter at a time across a row). `current_frame.segmentation` already gives you exact pixel counts, bounding boxes, and per-object hashes -- reading raw ASCII text character-by-character is slower, wastes far more output, and is more error-prone than one segmentation call.
- Do NOT re-derive the whole board layout from scratch every turn. Once you have summarized the board's objects, treat that summary as known on later turns -- reason only about what changed (diff `previous_frame`/`last_transition` against `current_frame`), not the full board again.
- Prefer acting over further exploration once you have a reasonable hypothesis: call `action(...)` with your best guess (batched into one call if you already know several steps) rather than continuing to analyze indefinitely -- you can course-correct from the result on the next turn.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometimes they might also be 1x1 tokens.
- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.

seg = current_frame.segmentation
nodes = seg['nodes']

# Get exact position of b and Y
for n in nodes:
    if n['color'] in ('b', 'Y'):
        b = n['boundary']
        rows = [p[0] for p in b]
        cols = [p[1] for p in b]
        print(f"color={n['color']} pixels={n['pixels']} bbox=({min(rows)},{min(cols)})-({max(rows)},{max(cols)})")
        # Print the actual boundary points
        print(f"  boundary points: {sorted(b)}")

# Look at the local area around b/Y (rows 35-42, cols 15-30)
ascii_lines = current_frame.ascii.split('\n')
print("\n--- Local area around b/Y (rows 35-42, cols 14-30) ---")
for r in range(35, 43):
    row_str = ascii_lines[r]
    print(f"Row {r}: ...{row_str[14:31]}...")

# Look at the gap area (rows 28-40, cols 40-55)
print("\n--- Gap area (rows 28-40, cols 40-55) ---")
for r in range(28, 41):
    row_str = ascii_lines[r]
    print(f"Row {r}: ...{row_str[40:56]}...")

# Check what objects are at rows 30-37 (the gap area)
print("\n--- Objects at rows 30-37 ---")
for n in nodes:
    b = n['boundary']
    rows = [p[0] for p in b]
    if min(rows) >= 30 and max(rows) <= 37:
        cols = [p[1] for p in b]
        if n['color'] not in ('B',):
            print(f"  id={n['id']} color={n['color']} pixels={n['pixels']} bbox=({min(rows)},{min(cols)})-({max(rows)},{max(cols)})")

# Check the S block structure - is b/Y inside S or in a separate pocket?
print("\n--- S block (id=90) details ---")
s_node = nodes[90]
print(f"  pixels={s_node['pixels']} children={s_node['children']}")
# Check if b/Y is adjacent to S
adj = seg['adjacency_list']
for n in nodes:
    if n['color'] == 'b':
        for pair in adj:
            if pair[0] == n['id'] or pair[1] == n['id']:
                other = pair[1] if pair[0] == n['id'] else pair[0]

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels, and new mechanics might be introduced.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still reliably clearing every level. Per level you are scored on action efficiency, `min(human_actions / agent_actions, 1.0)`, then squared, so once a level is solved every extra action hurts sharply.
- Use this as a test for false goals: if a value only ticks up by a fixed amount each action but never completes a level (a HUD counter, timer, or progress bar), it cannot be the objective; pursuing it means one wasted action per tick, the worst possible efficiency. The real goal is a change in the puzzle's own state that drives toward clearing the level.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is a lightweight frame view for the latest environment state.
- `current_frame` exposes only `.ascii`, `.step`, `.level`, `.shape`, and `.segmentation`.
- `current_frame.ascii` is a single newline-delimited string containing the latest board rendered with the letter-coded ARC color symbols.
- `current_frame.segmentation` parses the board into objects. It returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`.
- Each node in `segmentation['nodes']` is one 4-connected same-color object with: `id` (index, ordered top-most-left-most), `color` (ARC color character), `hash` (a signature of the object's color and shape that ignores its position -- equal hashes mean the same object regardless of where it is, so use it to track an object across frames or to spot multiple identical objects in one frame), `pixels` (cell count), `boundary` (clockwise outer-perimeter corner points as `[row, col]`), and `children` (ids of objects fully enclosed by this one).
- `segmentation['adjacency_list']` is a list of `[i, j]` node-id pairs whose objects share an edge.
- `current_frame.step` is the current environment step count.
- `current_frame.level` is the current level number.
- `current_frame.shape` is a `(rows, cols)` tuple.
- The raw numeric grid is intentionally not exposed. Use `current_frame.segmentation` as your primary view of the board -- objects, colors, shapes, containment, adjacency, and cross-frame object hashes. Use `current_frame.ascii` only to read a small, specific region; do not scan the whole board with it.
- `history` is a chronological list of action/frame snapshots.
- `history` is a Python list of objects, not a dict.
- Each history entry exposes only `.action` and `.frame`; entries are not subscriptable like `entry['action']`.
- Each `history[i].frame` is the frame after `history[i].action`; each frame exposes only `.ascii`, `.step`, `.level`, `.shape`, and `.segmentation`.
- Important history semantics: when `history` is non-empty, `history[-1].frame` is the same latest/post-action board as `current_frame`. It is not the previous board. To inspect the state before the latest action, use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` when available.
- `previous_frame` is the frame before the most recent real environment action, or `None` if no previous frame is available.
- `last_action` is the most recent real environment action name/display, or `None` before any real action.
- `last_action_frame` is the post-action frame for `last_action`; it matches `current_frame` after a real action.
- `transitions` is a chronological list of actual action transitions, excluding the initial seeded frame. Each transition exposes `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame` (alias of `.after_frame`), and `.result`.

- `last_action_result` is the persisted result dict from the most recent `action(...)` call. It remains available across later Python inspection calls that do not call `action(...)`, and is `{}` before any action result exists. Read transition metadata from fields/keys such as `last_action_result['board_changed']`, `last_action_result['done']`, `last_action_result['level_completed']`, `last_action_result['game_over']`, `last_action_result['reward']`, and `last_action_result['valid_actions']`.
- `valid_actions` is the current list of valid action names.
- Call `action(actions)` to execute one or more real environment actions from Python.
- Pass `action(actions)` a list like `['LEFT']` or `[{'action': 'MOUSE', 'row': 4, 'col': 7}]`.
- One action usually returns one frame, but a single action can result in a short multi-frame animation.
- After `action(actions)` returns, `current_frame`, `previous_frame`, `history`, `transitions`, `valid_actions`, and `last_action_result` are refreshed.
- Each result reports `board_changed`, plus `gameplay_changed` and `no_impact`: the harness learns the game's deterministic HUD/edge band (a moves-remaining or timer bar) online and tells you whether your action changed real gameplay (`gameplay_changed`) or only advanced that band (`no_impact` -- a wasted move, e.g. pressing into a wall). `hud_band` names the learned band when one exists (e.g. `rows 61-62`).
- `action(actions, intent=...)` defaults to `intent='explore'`: an explore action that turns out `no_impact` STOPS the rest of that batch (`stop_reason='no_impact_action'`) and returns so you re-ground, instead of wasting the queued actions on a plan the environment just disproved. Pass `intent='solve'` for a committed sequence you do not want interrupted by the no-impact check.
- The harness keeps a per-level STATE GRAPH of the canonical (HUD-excluded) boards you have visited and the actions between them. EVERY action result already gives you `state_node_id` (your node now), `graph_nodes` (count), and `untried_here` (the valid actions you have NOT yet taken from this state) -- so do NOT spend a tool-call turn re-deriving your position or local frontier; act on `untried_here` directly. The `state_graph` sandbox global is for PLANNING A ROUTE only (use it sparingly, not every turn): `.neighbors(id)`, `.tried_actions(id)`, `.distance(a,b)`, `.path(a,b)`, `.frontier()`, and `frame_from_state(id)` -> a FrameView (`.ascii`/`.segmentation`) to inspect a distant node before navigating to it.
- In `intent='explore'`, a whole action sequence that follows ONLY already-tried edges is REJECTED without executing (`stop_reason='known_action_rejected'`): it re-treads explored ground and reaches no new (state, action). You MAY route back through known / fully-explored states, but the plan must END with an untried action so it reaches new ground -- your frontier at a node is its `valid_actions` minus `state_graph.tried_actions(id)`. Use `intent='re-explore'` to deliberately traverse known states (the batch is only flagged, or hard-stopped in the strict A/B arm, if it reaches nothing new); `intent='solve'` bypasses the graph and no-impact checks entirely.
- Optional precision upgrade for that check: if you can pin down the deterministic HUD/bar exactly, write a PURE `advance_hud(frame, action) -> frame` (frame is the `.ascii` letter grid; return it with ONLY the bar advanced one step, everything else identical) and commit it in a `HUD model:` section with a fenced python code block. The harness re-verifies it against every action and, while its bar prediction holds, uses it (`no_impact_source: model`) instead of the statistical band -- you write it ONCE and it then runs free every turn with no further tokens. It only asks you to rewrite when it mispredicts (`hud_model: stale`, e.g. a refill or a new level). No HUD in this game? Don't write one -- the statistical fallback handles it (`no_impact_source: band` or `exact`).

- Every action in the result's `executed_actions` carries an `animation` record summarizing its intermediate frames (which are not visible in `current_frame`): `num_frames`, `changed`, `only_changed` (a transient flash that left the same final board), `total_cells_changed`, `cells_changed_per_frame` (per frame, where 0 is a held/dwell frame), and `objects` -- one string per connected animated region, e.g. `top-left (19,27) 4x4, 16 cells, per-frame [...]`, so two things moving at once (a box resizing while a light blinks) each keep a tight bounding box.

- `current_frame` is always the settled board after your last action -- on its own it is enough for a purely static reading, and you never have to look further.
- If you want to, `last_animation` additionally exposes every frame the engine produced for each INDIVIDUAL action of your most recent `action(...)` call: a list with one entry per action, in execution order, each with `.action` and `.frames` (intermediate animation frames plus the final frame; length 1 means that action caused no animation). After `action(['UP','UP','LEFT'])` there are three entries. All change within one entry's `.frames` is that single action animating -- read it to see per-move motion and causality that the settled frame discards.

Reach for it when an action reports animated objects, especially when a small `total_cells_changed` signals a focused, meaningful animation (a box growing, a token sliding) rather than a full-board redraw.
- `show_animation_by_bbox(action_index=0, row=..., col=..., rows=..., cols=...)` prints the same animation cropped to a region YOU choose (top-left `row`,`col` plus `rows`,`cols`) -- use it to inspect a spot the object split missed. Omit `rows`/`cols` to watch the FULL animation frames.
- It is entirely your judgement whether the intermediate frames are signal or noise. Motion may be meaningful (an object sliding, a chain reaction, a knock-on effect) OR incidental (a decorative transition, a flashing timer/HUD, a cosmetic redraw). Treat multi-frame motion as a HYPOTHESIS to test against the puzzle, not a fact. Reading `last_animation` costs tokens only when you inspect it, so pull it only when you expect it to help.

Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometimes they might also be 1x1 tokens.

If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. <parameter=code>
seg = current_frame.segmentation
nodes = seg['nodes']
adj = seg['adjacency_list']

# Build a quick lookup: for each cell, what object id is there
# Focus on the b/Y area - check pocket boundaries
ascii_lines = current_frame.ascii.split('\n')

# Print rows 36-41, cols 13-26 to see pocket walls
print("=== Pocket walls around b/Y (rows 36-41, cols 13-26) ===")
for r in range(36, 42):
    print(f"Row {r}: |{ascii_lines[r][13:27]}|")

# Check what's at the gap - rows 28-37, cols 42-50
print("\n=== Gap structure (rows 28-37, cols 42-50) ===")
for r in range(28, 38):
    print(f"Row {r}: |{ascii_lines[r][42:51]}|")

# What objects are adjacent to b?
b_id = None
for n in nodes:
    if n['color'] == 'b':
        b_id = n['id']
        break

print(f"\n=== Adjacency for b (id={b_id}) ===")
for pair in adj:
    if pair[0] == b_id or pair[1] == b_id:
        other = pair[1] if pair[0] == b_id else pair[0]
        on = nodes[other]
        print(f"  adjacent to id={other} color={on['color']} pixels={on['pixels']}")

# Check history - what changed in last action?
if history:
    print(f"\n=== Last action: {history[-1].action} ===")
    if len(history) >= 2:
        prev_seg = history[-2].frame.segmentation
        prev_nodes = prev_seg['nodes']
        # Compare b/Y positions
        for pn in prev_nodes:
            if pn['color'] in ('b', 'Y'):
                print(f"  BEFORE: color={pn['color']} pixels={pn['pixels']} bbox=({pn['boundary'][0][0]},{pn['boundary'][0][1]})-({max(p[0] for p in pn['boundary'])},{max(p[1] for p in pn['boundary'])})")
        for n in nodes:
            if n['color'] in ('b', 'Y'):
                print(f"  AFTER:  color={n['color']} pixels={n['pixels']} bbox=({n['boundary'][0][0]},{n['boundary'][0][1]})-({max(p[0] for p in n['boundary'])},{max(p[1] for p in n['boundary'])})")

# Count p's at bottom row
bottom_row = ascii_lines[63]
p_count = bottom_row.count('p')
print(f"\n=== Bottom row p count: {p_count} ===")
print(f"Bottom row: {bottom_row[:20]}...{bottom_row[-20:]}")

# Check the gap - is it a vertical strip of S blocks?
print("\n=== Gap column check (cols 44-48, rows 28-37) ===")
for r in range(28, 38):
    chars = ascii_lines[r][44:49]
    print(f"Row {r}: |{chars}|")

- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels, and new mechanics might be introduced.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still reliably clearing every level. Per level you are scored on action efficiency, `min(human_actions / agent_actions, 1.0)`, then squared, so once a level is solved every extra action hurts sharply.
- Use this as a test for false goals: if a value only ticks up by a fixed amount each action but never completes a level (a HUD counter, timer, or progress bar), it cannot be the objective; pursuing it means one wasted action per tick, the worst possible efficiency. The real goal is a change in the puzzle's own state that drives toward clearing the level.

Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is a lightweight frame view for the latest environment state.
- `current_frame` exposes only `.ascii`, `.step`, `.level`, `.shape`, and `.segmentation`.
- `current_frame.ascii` is a single newline-delimited string containing the latest board rendered with the letter-coded ARC color symbols.
- `current_frame.segmentation` parses the board into objects. It returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`.
- Each node in `segmentation['nodes']` is one 4-connected same-color object with: `id` (index, ordered top-most-left-most), `color` (ARC color character), `hash` (a signature of the object's color and shape that ignores its position -- equal hashes mean the same object regardless of where it is, so use it to track an object across frames or to spot multiple identical objects in one frame), `pixels` (cell count), `boundary` (clockwise outer-perimeter corner points as `[row, col]`), and `children` (ids of objects fully enclosed by this one).
- `segmentation['adjacency_list']` is a list of `[i, j]` node-id pairs whose objects share an edge.
- `current_frame.step` is the current environment step count.
- `current_frame.level` is the current level number.
- `current_frame.shape` is a `(rows, cols)` tuple.
- The raw numeric grid is intentionally not exposed. Use `current_frame.segmentation` as your primary view of the board -- objects, colors, shapes, containment, adjacency, and cross-frame object hashes. Use `current_frame.ascii` only to read a small, specific region; do not scan the whole board with it.
- `history` is a chronological list of action/frame snapshots.
- `history` is a Python list of objects, not a dict.
- Each history entry exposes only `.action` and `.frame`; entries are not subscriptable like `entry['action']`.
- Each `history[i].frame` is the frame after `history[i].action`; each frame exposes only `.ascii`, `.step`, `.level`, `.shape`, and `.segmentation`.
- Important history semantics: when `history` is non-empty, `history[-1].frame` is the same latest/post-action board as `current_frame`. It is not the previous board. To inspect the state before the latest action, use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` when available.

It remains available across later Python inspection calls that do not call `action(...)`, and is `{}` before any action result exists. Read transition metadata from fields/keys such as `last_action_result['board_changed']`, `last_action_result['done']`, `last_action_result['level_completed']`, `last_action_result['game_over']`, `last_action_result['reward']`, and `last_action_result['valid_actions']`.

- After `action(actions)` returns, `current_frame`, `previous_frame`, `history`, `transitions`, `valid_actions`, and `last_action_result` are refreshed.
- Each result reports `board_changed`, plus `gameplay_changed` and `no_impact`: the harness learns the game's deterministic HUD/edge band (a moves-remaining or timer bar) online and tells you whether your action changed real gameplay (`gameplay_changed`) or only advanced that band (`no_impact` -- a wasted move, e.g. pressing into a wall). `hud_band` names the learned band when one exists (e.g. `rows 61-62`).
- `action(actions, intent=...)` defaults to `intent='explore'`: an explore action that turns out `no_impact` STOPS the rest of that batch (`stop_reason='no_impact_action'`) and returns so you re-ground, instead of wasting the queued actions on a plan the environment just disproved. Pass `intent='solve'` for a committed sequence you do not want interrupted by the no-impact check.
- The harness keeps a per-level STATE GRAPH of the canonical (HUD-excluded) boards you have visited and the actions between them. EVERY action result already gives you `state_node_id` (your node now), `graph_nodes` (count), and `untried_here` (the valid actions you have NOT yet taken from this state) -- so do NOT spend a tool-call turn re-deriving your position or local frontier; act on `untried_here` directly. The `state_graph` sandbox global is for PLANNING A ROUTE only (use it sparingly, not every turn): `.neighbors(id)`, `.tried_actions(id)`, `.distance(a,b)`, `.path(a,b)`, `.frontier()`, and `frame_from_state(id)` -> a FrameView (`.ascii`/`.segmentation`) to inspect a distant node before navigating to it.
- In `intent='explore'`, a whole action sequence that follows ONLY already-tried edges is REJECTED without executing (`stop_reason='known_action_rejected'`): it re-treads explored ground and reaches no new (state, action). You MAY route back through known / fully-explored states, but the plan must END with an untried action so it reaches new ground -- your frontier at a node is its `valid_actions` minus `state_graph.tried_actions(id)`. Use `intent='re-explore'` to deliberately traverse known states (the batch is only flagged, or hard-stopped in the strict A/B arm, if it reaches nothing new); `intent='solve'` bypasses the graph and no-impact checks entirely.

The harness re-verifies it against every action and, while its bar prediction holds, uses it (`no_impact_source: model`) instead of the statistical band -- you write it ONCE and it then runs free every turn with no further tokens. It only asks you to rewrite when it mispredicts (`hud_model: stale`, e.g. a refill or a new level). No HUD in this game? - `current_frame` is always the settled board after your last action -- on its own it is enough for a purely static reading, and you never have to look further.
- If you want to, `last_animation` additionally exposes every frame the engine produced for each INDIVIDUAL action of your most recent `action(...)` call: a list with one entry per action, in execution order, each with `.action` and `.frames` (intermediate animation frames plus the final frame; length 1 means that action caused no animation). After `action(['UP','UP','LEFT'])` there are three entries. All change within one entry's `.frames` is that single action animating -- read it to see per-move motion and causality that the settled frame discards.
- `frame_stats` summarizes how much THIS game has animated so far: `actions`, `animated_actions`, `mean_frames_per_action`, `max_frames`, and `recent_frame_counts` (per action, most recent last). Use it to gauge, from the game so far, whether the extra frames are likely worth reading.
- `show_animation_by_objects(action_index=0)` prints that action's animation one object at a time, each cropped to its own bounding box (labeled with the object's top-left row, col) and reduced to its distinct states -- small and legible even for a long animation. Reach for it when an action reports animated objects, especially when a small `total_cells_changed` signals a focused, meaningful animation (a box growing, a token sliding) rather than a full-board redraw.
- `show_animation_by_bbox(action_index=0, row=..., col=..., rows=..., cols=...)` prints the same animation cropped to a region YOU choose (top-left `row`,`col` plus `rows`,`cols`) -- use it to inspect a spot the object split missed. Omit `rows`/`cols` to watch the FULL animation frames.
- It is entirely your judgement whether the intermediate frames are signal or noise. Motion may be meaningful (an object sliding, a chain reaction, a knock-on effect) OR incidental (a decorative transition, a flashing timer/HUD, a cosmetic redraw). Treat multi-frame motion as a HYPOTHESIS to test against the puzzle, not a fact. Reading `last_animation` costs tokens only when you inspect it, so pull it only when you expect it to help.

Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometimes they might also be 1x1 tokens.
- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
